security. Specifically, a key-policy attribute-based encryption scheme is said to be fully secure if for every stateful PPT adversary A, there exists a negligible function

negl(\cdot)
, such that for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct})=b:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\\
((m_{0},m_{1}),x)\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mpk})\\
b\leftarrow\{0,1\},\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x,m_{b})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\mathcal{A}
is admissible if all query
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
made to oracle
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
satisfies
f(x)=0
.

It is selectively securre if
\mathcal{A}
needs to output
x
before seeing
\mathsf{mpk}
.

Definition C.3

(
\gamma
-Unremovability)
.

The
\gamma
-Unremovability for a watermarkable ABE scheme
says, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, and for all PPT admissible stateful adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
such that:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{aux}=(m_{0},m_{1},x),C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}\\
\wedge C\text{ is }\gamma\text{-good}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\{m_{0},m_{1}\},x,C)\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mpk})\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
{\mathcal{Q}}
is the set of marks queried by
\mathcal{A}
and
C
is a PPT admissible, stateful
\gamma
-good adversary in the security game
G_{\mathsf{ABE}}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{aux}=(m_{0},m_{1},x),\cdot)
, more specifically:

\Pr\left[C^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct})=b:\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},m_{b},x),b\leftarrow\{0,1\}\end{array}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\gamma.

\mathcal{A},C
is admissible if all query
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
made to oracle
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
satisfies
f(x)=0
.

C
will be given the
\mathsf{KeyGen}
oracle as
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\mathsf{Unmarked})
where it only queries on unmarked keys.

A selective variant is requiring
\mathcal{A}
output
x
before seeing
\mathsf{mpk}
.

Remark C.4
.

As discussed in
Section

5.5.2
, the above definition is equivalent to letting
C
output
(m_{0},m_{1})
because we can view any distribution over
(m_{0},m_{1})
used by
C
as a convex combination of different message pairs
\{(m_{0},m_{1})_{i}\})_{i}
and its corresponding strategy such that the overall winning probability is
1/2+\gamma
. Thus, we can let
\mathcal{A}
pick the message-pair and corresponding strategy with the largest winning probability and hardcode them into
C
instead.

But
x
needs to be output by
\mathcal{A}
instead of
C
, because the
\mathsf{Extract}
algorithm does not get to see queries of
\mathcal{A}
and thus cannot make sure if
f(x)=0
for all
f
queried.

Remark C.5

(Modification of
[
GKM+19
]
Watermarkable ABE scheme)
.

In
[
GKM+19
]
, the unremovability game allows
\mathcal{A}
to be given only one policy-embedded key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and then allowed to query
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk}_{f},\cdot)
for polynomially many times. In our setting, we let
\mathcal{A}
query a marked master secret key
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
and the
\mathsf{KeyGen}
algorithm can derive a functional key from a "marked" master secret key.
We will show how to adapt
[
GKM+19
]
’s construction for our use.

C.2
Preliminaries: Delegatable ABE and Mixed FE

The
[
GKM+19
]
’s watermakable PKE and ABE building blocks are delegatable
\mathsf{ABE}
and mixed
\mathsf{FE}
.

C.2.1
Delegatable ABE

Delegatable ABE has the same syntax with ABE (see
Section

C.1
, ignoring the extraction key, marking key and
\mathsf{Extract},\mathsf{Mark}
algorithm) with an additional algorithm
\mathsf{Delegate}
:

\mathsf{Delegate}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},g)\to sk_{f,g}
: on input
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and a predicate
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
, output a delegated key
\mathsf{sk}_{f,g}
.

Correctness of Delegation

There exists a negligible funcion
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)

such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
f,g\in{\mathcal{F}},m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, when
f(x)=g(x)=1
the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f,g},\mathsf{ct})=m:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda}),\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f,g}\leftarrow\mathsf{Delegate}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},g)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x,m)\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

Delegatable ABE Security

The
\mathsf{DABE}
security is the same as
\mathsf{ABE}
security except that the adversary
\mathcal{A}
queries an oracle
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
that has several modes and takes in a query of the form
(f,\mathsf{index},\mathsf{mode})
:

•

If
\mathsf{mode}=\mathsf{Storekey}
, the challenger generates a new key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)
and store the generated
(n,f,\mathsf{sk}_{f})
where
n
is an index; update
n:=n+1
.

•

If
\mathsf{mode}=\mathsf{OutputKey}
: the challenger first checks if there exists a key tuple of the form
(\mathsf{index},g,sk_{g})
. If no such tuple exists or if
g(x)=1
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it replies with

(\mathsf{index},sk_{g})
.

•

If
\mathsf{mode}=\mathsf{DelegateKey}
, then the challenger first checks if there exists a key tuple of the form

(\mathsf{index},g,sk_{g})
. If no such tuple exists or if
g(x)=f(x)=1
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it generates

sk_{g,f}\leftarrow\mathsf{Delegate}(\mathsf{sk}_{g},f)
and replies with
(\mathsf{index},sk_{g,f})
.

C.2.2
Mixed Functional Encryption

An
\mathsf{MFE}
scheme consists of the following algorithms:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})
: on input security parameter
\lambda
, outputs the public parameters/master public key
\mathsf{mpk}
and master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk})\to\mathsf{ct}
: on master public key, the normal encryption outputs ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},f)\to\mathsf{ct}
: on master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and fucnction
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},m)\to\mathsf{sk}_{m}
: on master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and message
m
, outputs a key
\mathsf{sk}_{m}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{m},\mathsf{ct})\to\{0,1\}
: on secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{m}
and ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, outputs a bit.

Correctness

A mixed functionl encryption scheme is correct if there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},f\in{\mathcal{F}},m\in{\mathcal{M}}
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{m},\mathsf{ct})=1:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda}),\mathsf{sk}_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},m)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk})\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{m},\mathsf{ct})=f(m):\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda}),\mathsf{sk}_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},m)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},f)\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

q
-Bounded function indistinguishability

Let
q=q(\lambda)
be any fixed polynomial. A mixed
functional encryption scheme is said to satisfy
q
-bounded function indistinguishability security if for every stateful PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there
exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
, such that for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct})=b:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(f_{0},f_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mpk})\\
b\leftarrow\{0,1\},\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{mpk},f_{b})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

where
\mathcal{A}
can make at most
q
queries to
\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle; every secret key query
m
made by adversary
\mathcal{A}
to the
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle must satisfy
f_{0}(m)=f_{1}(m)
.

q
-Bounded accept indistinguishability

Let
q=q(\lambda)
be any fixed polynomial. A mixed
functional encryption scheme is said to satisfy
q
-bounded accept indistinguishability security if for every stateful PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there
exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
, such that for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct}_{b})=b:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
f^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mpk})\\
b\leftarrow\{0,1\},\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},f^{*}),\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

where
\mathcal{A}
can make at most
q
queries to
\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle; every secret key query
m
made by adversary
\mathcal{A}
to the
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle must satisfy
f^{*}(m)=1
.

\mathsf{QTrace}
Jump-Finding Algorithm

The
[
GKM+19
]
watermarkable ABE schemes
\mathsf{Extract}
procedure also additionally uses an algorithm
\mathsf{QTrace}
that is widely used in the traitor-tracing literature.

\mathsf{QTrace}
on inpput parameters
(\lambda,N,q,\delta,\gamma)
and given black box access to a program
Q
, runs in time
t={\sf poly}(\lambda,\log N,q,1/\delta)
. Since detailed discussions are not essential to our presentation on the modified scheme, we refer the readers to
[
GKM+19
]
Section 4.2.3 for details.

C.3
Construction

Now we give the construction for watermarkable implementation of ABE, which is modified from
[
GKM+19
]
Section 4.3.

•

Given a delegatable
\mathsf{ABE}
scheme
\mathsf{DABE}=(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Dec},\\
\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Delegate})

and a
\mathsf{MFE}
scheme
(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{SKEnc},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
be the marked space,
\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}
the attribute space, and
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathsf{Functions}[\mathcal{X},\{0,1\}]
be thepredicate class for the target watermarkable ABE scheme, with parameters
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{Y}=\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}+\kappa}
and
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{C}}\cup\{\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Dec}\}\cup\{\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Dec}\wedge C\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}
.

•

the
\mathsf{DABE}
scheme has attribute class
\mathcal{Y}
, message space
{\mathcal{M}}
, predicate class
cD
. Elements in
\mathcal{Y}
hav the following format
(x\in\mathcal{X},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{ct})
.

•

Let
\gamma
be the unremovability parameter.

\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{xk})
:

1.

compute
\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
;

2.

compute
(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
;

3.

output
\mathsf{mpk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk});\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk});

\mathsf{mk}=\mathsf{xk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk})
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f,\mathsf{mode},\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:
Let
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk})

If
\mathsf{mode}=\mathsf{Unmarked}
:

1.

Let
\tilde{f}:\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}+\kappa}\to\{0,1\}
denote the predicate
\tilde{f}(x,c)=f(x),x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}},c\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
.

2.

output
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\tilde{f})
.

If
\mathsf{mode}=\mathsf{Marked}
:

1.

compute
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\tau)

2.

Let
g_{\tau}

denote the mixed FE decryption circuit with
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}
hardwired, i.e.
g_{\tau}=\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\tau},\cdot)
;

3.

compute
\mathsf{sk}_{\tilde{g_{\tau}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk},\tilde{g_{\tau}})
where
\tilde{g_{\tau}}(x,c)=g_{\tau}(c),\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}},c\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
;

4.

compute and output delegated key
\mathsf{sk}_{f,\tilde{g_{\tau}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Delegate}(\mathsf{sk}_{\tilde{g_{\tau}}},f)

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x,m)
:

Let
\mathsf{mpk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk});
compute
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk})
.

Next compute ad output
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},(x,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}),m)
where
(x,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}})
is the attribute.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})\to m/\bot
: output
m\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\tau)
:

1.

\mathsf{mk}:=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk})
;

2.

compute
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk})

3.

Let
g_{\tau}

denote the mixed FE decryption circuit with
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}
hardwired, i.e.
g_{\tau}=\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\tau},\cdot)
;

4.

compute and output
\mathsf{sk}_{\tilde{g_{\tau}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk},\tilde{g_{\tau}})
where
\tilde{g_{\tau}}(x,c)=g_{\tau}(c),\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}},c\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa}

\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{aux}=(x,m_{0},m_{1}),C,q)
:

Let
\mathsf{xk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk});\mathsf{mpk}=(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk})
;

The extraction algorithms runs the
\mathsf{QTrace}
algorithm as
\tau\leftarrow\mathsf{QTrace}^{Q_{C}}(\lambda,2^{\ell_{1}},q,\delta,\gamma)
where
q
is the bounded parameter for the
\mathsf{MFE}
scheme (upper bound on the number of queries to the
\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracles),
\gamma
is the unremovability paramter and
\delta=\gamma/(5+2\ell_{1}q)
.
Q_{C}
is simulated as follows:

On input
\tau\leftarrow[0,2^{\ell_{1}}]
:

•

compute
\mathsf{MFE}
ciphertext as
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{comp}_{\tau})
, where
\mathsf{comp}_{\tau}
is the comparison function that on input
z
, output 1 if and only if
z\geq\tau
.

•

sample a bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and compute
\mathsf{ct}_{b}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},(x,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}),m_{b})
where
(x,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}})
is the attribute input.

•

compute
b^{\prime}\leftarrow C^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\mathsf{Unmarked})}(\mathsf{ct})
and output 1 if
b^{\prime}=b
and 0 otherwise.
C
has oracle access to
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\mathsf{Unmarked})
, which can be simulated using
\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk}
provided in
\mathsf{xk}
.

Correctness and Other Properties

The correctness of decryption, correctness of extraction, functionality-preserving and ABE security can directly follow the same proof as in
[
GKM+19
]
so we omit them here.

We can observe that the above scheme also satisfies the additional extraction key simulation property and extraction syntax also satisfy our requirement on watermarkable implementation: the extraction key has
\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk}
embedded so enough to simulate the
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\mathsf{Unmarked})
queries for program
C
. The entire extraction algorithm follows the format that runs the simulated stage-2 security game for
\mathsf{ABE}
.

Security Proof

The security proof will be similar to the proof in
[
GKM+19
]
. Overall, our changes are minor.
To avoid repetitive work of doing a same proof verbatimly, we omit the parts of our proof which are exactly the same as
[
GKM+19
]
and refer the readers to Section 4.3.1 of
[
GKM+19
]
"Proof of Theorem 4.15(Unremovability)" part.
We only discuss the major changes to make to adjust to our construction:

All discussions and lemmas before Lemma 4.18 will be exactly the same when moved to our case, except some minor changes:

1.

We adjust Experiment
\mathsf{GetCircuit}_{\mathcal{A}}(\lambda)
in Figure 2 of
[
GKM+19
]
to fit our unremovability game:
\mathcal{A}
is allowed to query
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
for arbitrarily polynomial times. We will discuss how this oracle is simulated when doing different reductions.

2.

In Lemma 4.16 and 4.17 invokes security of
\mathsf{MFE}
: in our setting, the reduction algorithm will additionally query the
\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle when dealing with the adversary
\mathcal{A}
s
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\mathsf{Marked},\cdot)
queries. But these queries have exactly the same format as when answering the marking queries
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\tau)
. Moreover, in both the
q
-bounded function indistinguishability and accept indistinguishability games, the number of queries a reduction can make to oracle
\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
are unbounded polynomial.
Therefore, the proof of Lemma 4.16 and 4.17 are unaffected.

We mainly discuss the changes to Lemma 4.18 and take some steps from
[
GKM+19
]
verbatimly for completeness:
Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be the reduction to security of
\mathsf{DABE}
.

1.

Adversary
\mathcal{A}
chooses a challenge attribute
x
and sends it over to
{\mathcal{B}}
, since we consider only selective security.

2.

The DABE challenger samples a
\mathsf{DABE}
key pair
(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
. Algorithm
{\mathcal{B}}
samples a mixed FE
key pair
(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
and sends
\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk}
to
{\mathcal{B}}
.
, and sends the public key
\mathsf{mpk}=(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

3.

{\mathcal{B}}
computes
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{comp}_{2^{\ell}_{1}})
and commits to the challenge attribute
x^{*}=(x,\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{\mathsf{MFE}})
.

4.

When
\mathcal{A}
makes a query to the
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracles,
{\mathcal{B}}
simulates the answer as follows:

(a)

If
\mathcal{A}
makes a marking query on message
\tau
:
{\mathcal{B}}
computes the function
\tilde{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}}
as described in the construction on its own; using
\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}
; then it sends query
(\tilde{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}},\bot,\mathsf{StoreKey})
to
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
.

Note that
\tau\in[0,2^{\ell_{1}}]
, so
\tilde{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}}(x^{*})=0
is satisfied with overwhelming probability.

{\mathcal{B}}
gets reply in the form
(\mathsf{index},\bot)
;
{\mathcal{B}}
then makes another query
(\tilde{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}},\mathsf{index},\mathsf{OutputKey})

and gets back
(\mathsf{index},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{sk}_{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}})
.

{\mathcal{B}}
makes a table that stores the following information
(\mathsf{index},\tau,\mathsf{sk}_{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}})
in each entry.

(b)

If
\mathcal{A}
makes
\mathsf{KeyGen}
query of the format
(\mathsf{Marked},\tau,f)
:
{\mathcal{B}}
first checks if an entry containing
\tau
is stored in its table in the format
(\mathsf{index},\tau,\mathsf{sk}_{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}}})
; if so
{\mathcal{B}}
prepares
\tilde{f}(x,c)=f(x)
and queries the
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle on input
(\mathsf{index},f,\mathsf{DelegateKey})
and gets back
(\mathsf{index},\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{sk}_{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}},\tilde{f}})
.
{\mathcal{B}}
sends
\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{sk}_{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}},\tilde{f}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

If there is no such entry,
{\mathcal{B}}
will query
(\tilde{g_{\mathsf{sk}_{\tau^{\prime}}}},\bot,\mathsf{StoreKey})
first and then query
(\mathsf{index}^{\prime},f,\mathsf{DelegateKey})
to get the same output.

(c)

If
\mathcal{A}
makes a
\mathsf{KeyGen}
query in the format

(\mathsf{Unmarked},f)
, then

{\mathcal{B}}
prepares
\tilde{f}(x,c)=f(x)
and first queries
(\tilde{f},\bot,\mathsf{StoreKey})
to get back some
(\mathsf{index}_{\tilde{f}},\bot)
; then queries again
(\mathsf{index}_{\tilde{f}},\tilde{f},\mathsf{OutputKey})
for
sk_{\tilde{f}}
.
Note it is also satisfied that
\tilde{f}(x^{*})=f(x)=0
.

5.

At the end of the game,
\mathcal{A}
outputs a pair of messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
and a circuit
C^{*}
.

6.

{\mathcal{B}}
then sends
(m_{0},m_{1})
to DABE challenger and gets a ciphrtext
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
.
{\mathcal{B}}
samples a random bit
\beta\leftarrow\{0,1\}

and computes a fresh ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{DABE}.\mathsf{mpk},(x,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}),m_{\beta})
, where
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{MFE}}\leftarrow\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{SKEnc}(\mathsf{MFE}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{comp}_{2^{\ell_{1}}})
.

C^{*}
will continue to make queries but only allowed to query
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{Unmarked},\cdot)
oracle.
{\mathcal{B}}
can answer these queries by querying
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
with
(\tilde{f},\bot,\mathsf{StoreKey})
, because
{\mathcal{B}}
is allowed to make such queries in
\mathsf{DABE}
game:

•

If
C
makes a
\mathsf{KeyGen}
query in the format

(\mathsf{Unmarked},f)
, then

{\mathcal{B}}
prepares
\tilde{f}(x,c)=f(x)
and first queries
(\tilde{f},\bot,\mathsf{StoreKey})
to get back some
(\mathsf{index}_{\tilde{f}},\bot)
; then queries again
(\mathsf{index}_{\tilde{f}},\tilde{f},\mathsf{OutputKey})
for
sk_{\tilde{f}}
.
Note it is also satisfied that
\tilde{f}(x^{*})=f(x)=0
.

7.

Finally,
{\mathcal{B}}
runs the decryption circuit
C^{*}
on
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
and
\mathsf{ct}
, and
if
C^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{Unmarked},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct}^{*})=C^{\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{Unmarked},\cdot)}(\mathsf{ct})
, it outputs
b^{\prime}=\beta
. Otherwise, it outputs
b^{\prime}=1-\beta
.

The rest of the analysis is the same as in the original proof.

Appendix D
Watermarkable Implementation of Digital Signatures

D.1
Preliminaries: Constrained Signatures

The building block of a bounded collusion resistant watermarkable implementation of digital signatures in
[
GKM+19
]
relies on the following building block.

Constrained Signatures

A constrained signature with message space
{\mathcal{M}}
and consraint family
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathsf{Function}[{\mathcal{M}},\{0,1\}]
is a tuple of algorithms:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk})
. On input the security parameter
\lambda
, the setup algorithm outputs the verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m)\to{\sf sig}
. On input the master signing key
\mathsf{msk}
and a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the signing
algorithm outputs a signature
{\sf sig}
.

\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,{\sf sig})\to b
. On input the verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, and a signature
{\sf sig}
, the
verification algorithm outputs a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
.

\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{msk},f)\to\mathsf{sk}_{f}
: On input the master signing key
\mathsf{msk}
and a function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, the constrain
algorithm outputs a constrained key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
.

\mathsf{ConstrainSign}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},m)\to{\sf sig}
. On input a constrained key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the signing
algorithm outputs a signature
{\sf sig}
.

Correctness

A constrained signature scheme is correct if for all messages
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and key pair

(vk,msk)\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

\Pr[\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m))=1]=1.

In addition, for all constraints
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
where
f(m)=1
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,\mathsf{ConstrainSign}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},m))=1:\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{msk},f)]=1.

Constrained Unforgeability

A constrained signature scheme is secure if for every stateful admissible PPT
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
such that for all
\lambda in\mathbb{N}
:

\Pr\left[\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m^{*},{\sf sig}^{*})=1:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(m^{*},{\sf sig}^{*})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{vk})\\
\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
\mathcal{A}
is admissible if (1) it does not make a signing query on message
m^{*}

; and (2)
it does not make a constrained key query for any function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
f(m^{*})=1
.

D.2
Definition: Watermarkable Signatures

A watermarkable signatures scheme consists of the following algorithms:

•

\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})
: on input security parameter outputs verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, signing key
\mathsf{sk}
; extraction key
\mathsf{xk}
, marking key
\mathsf{mk}
.

•

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}
. On input the marking key
\mathsf{mk}
, a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
, and a mark
\tau\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, the
marking algorithm outputs a marked key
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}
.

•

{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},m)\to{\sf sig}
. On input a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the signing
algorithm outputs a signature
{\sf sig}
.

\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,\sigma)\to 0/1
. On input a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, and a signature
{\sf sig}
, the
verification algorithm outputs a bit to signify whether the signature is valid or not.

•

\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{vk},C)\to\tau/\bot
. On input the extraction key
\mathsf{xk}
, a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, and a circuit

C
, the extraction algorithm either outputs a mark
\tau\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
or
\bot
.

The correctness, meaningfulness and other properties are natural to derive from the general watermarking definition
Section

4.1
. We refer to
[
GKM+19
]
for more details and only present functionality-preserving and security defintions.

Functionality Preserving

A watermarkable signature scheme satisfies
the functionality-preserving property if there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
such that for all

\lambda\in\mathbb{N},(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{xk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})
,
m\in{\mathcal{M}},\tau\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,\mathsf{wSecEval}(\mathsf{sk}_{\tau},m))=1:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\tau)\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

We provide the unremovability definition, which is different from the one defined in
[
GKM+19
]
, but their scheme can be modified to satisfy this definition.

\gamma
-Unremovability

For every stateful
\gamma
-unremovable admissible PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a
negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, the following holds:

\Pr\left[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{vk},C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}\\
\wedge C\text{ is }\gamma\text{-good}:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\\
C\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{vk})\\
\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
{\mathcal{Q}}
is the set of marks queried by
\mathcal{A}
and
C
is said to be a (PPT admissible, stateful)
\gamma
-good adversary if:

\Pr\left[C^{{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)}\to(m^{*},{\sf sig}^{*}):\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m^{*},{\sf sig}^{*})=1\end{array}\right]\geq\gamma.

C
is admissible if and only if it does not query
{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
on
m^{*}
.

Discussions on Security Definitions and
[
GKM+19
]
construction

In the
[
GKM+19
]
’s watermarkable signature scheme, the unmarked
{\sf Sign}
function and the circuit used to compute signatures using marked keys have different output distributions, if we let
\mathcal{A}
query
{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
oracle in the first stage but allow
C
to choose the challenge message to sign on, then there can be an attack. Because the
[
GKM+19
]
scheme ’s
{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
and marked signing circuit have different output distributions. If
C
can choose its own
m^{*}
but the
\mathsf{Extract}
algorithm does not know which queries
\mathcal{A}
has made in stage 1, then
C
can be hardcoded with some
m^{*},{\sf sig}^{\prime}
where watermark cannot be extracted. We thus give the above definition, which suffice for our applications 1.

D.3
Construction

We show the following modified construction from
[
GKM+19
]
based on a constrained signature scheme
\mathsf{CSig}
defined in
Section

D.1
:

•

Let
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{T}}\cup\{\bot\}
. For a mark
\tau^{*}\in{\mathcal{T}}
, let
f_{\tau^{*}}:{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{M}}\to\{0,1\}
be the function
f_{\tau^{*}}(\tau,m)=1
if
\tau=\tau^{*}
and 0 otherwise.

\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{xk})
:
outputs a signing/verification key-pair
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
;
\mathsf{mk}=\bot,\mathsf{xk}=\mathsf{sk}
.

{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},m)\to{\sf sig}
:
On input a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
, and a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the signing algorithm signs
{\sf sig}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{CSig}.{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},(\bot,m))
, and outputs the signature
{\sf sig}=(\bot,{\sf sig}^{\prime})
.

\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},m,{\sf sig})\to b
. On input a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, a message
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, and a signature
{\sf sig}=(\tau^{\prime},{\sf sig}^{\prime})
; the verification algorithm outputs
b\leftarrow\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},(\tau^{\prime},m),{\sf sig}^{\prime})
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\tau)\to C
. On input a marking key
\mathsf{mk}=\bot
, a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
, and a mark
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, the
marking algorithm computes
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{sk},f_{\tau})
and outputs a circuit
C_{\tau}:{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathcal{SIG}
where
C_{\tau}(\cdot):=(\tau,\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{ConstrainSign}(\mathsf{sk}_{\tau},(\tau,\cdot)))
.

\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{vk},C)\to\tau/\bot
: on
\mathsf{xk}=\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{vk}
and circuit
C:{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathcal{SIG}
, perform the following for
T=\lambda/\gamma
times where
\gamma
is the unremovability parameter:

•

For
i\in[T]
: compute
(m_{i}=(\tau_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}),{\sf sig}_{i})\leftarrow C^{{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)}
. If
\mathsf{CSig}.\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{vk},(\tau_{i},m_{i}^{\prime}),{\sf sig}_{i})=1
and
m_{i}^{\prime}
has not been queried, abort and output
\tau_{i}^{\prime}
.

The correctness, functionlity-preserving, meaningfulness proof will all follow exactly from
[
GKM+19
]
.
Even though our extraction algorithm is different, the unremovability proof will follow similarly: any valid signatures provided by
C
must contain some
\tau_{i}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
of the marking queries, otherwise it helps break the constrained unforgeable security of the constrained signature.
The only change is that the reduction
does not answer any
{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
queries in the first stage, and thus do not need to query
\mathsf{CSig}.{\sf Sign}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
oracle when interacting with
\mathcal{A}
, but only when interacting with the circuit
C
produced by
\mathcal{A}
.

Appendix E
Watermarkable CCA-secure Hybrid Encryption (Key Encapsulation Scheme)

Theorem E.1
.

Assuming LWE, there exists secure watermarkable implementation of a CCA-secure hybrid encryption scheme.

The assumption of LWE comes from the need of watermarkable CCA-secure PKE.

Construction

A watermarkable implementation of CCA-secure hybrid encryption
\mathsf{wHE}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\\
\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Extract})
can be built from a CCA-secure secret key encryption scheme
\mathsf{SKE}=(\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec})
and a watermarkable implementatin of CCA-secure public key encryption
\mathsf{wPKE}=(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{WMSetup},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Extract})
as follows:

•

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda):
compute
(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{mk})\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{WMSetup}(\lambda)
. Output
\mathsf{sk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk};\mathsf{pk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk};\mathsf{xk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk};\mathsf{mk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{mk}

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)
: compute
\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
; then compute
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{sk})
; compute
\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{sk},m)
. Output
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
.

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})
: parse
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
; compute
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
and then
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
. Output
m^{\prime}
.

•

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\tau)
: parse
\mathsf{mk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{mk};\mathsf{sk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk}
; output
\mathsf{sk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{sk},\tau)
;

•

\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{aux}=\bot,C)
:

–

parse
\mathsf{xk}:=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk};\mathsf{pk}:=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}
. Create the following circuit
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
with black-box access to
C
:

*

C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
is hardcoded with
\mathsf{pk}
and simulates the CCA-PKE game for
C
as follows:

*

give
\mathsf{pk}
to
C
; for
C
’s decryption queries
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
: query an external oracle
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
;
output
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
.

*

C
submits challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
submits
(\mathsf{sk}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}),\mathsf{sk}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}))
to the external challenger;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
receives challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
from the challenger and
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
sends

\mathsf{ct}^{**}=(\mathsf{ct}^{*},\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},m_{b}))
to
C
,
where
m_{b}\leftarrow\{m_{0},m_{1}\}
.

*

C
continues to simulate the decryption oracle as above to decrypt only valid ciphertexts.

*

If
C
outputs
b^{\prime}=b
, then
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
outputs guess
0
; else it outputs guess 1.

–

output
\tau/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{aux}=\bot,C_{\mathsf{PKE}})
.

Now we prove
Theorem

E.1

Proof.

Suppose there exists adversary
\mathcal{A}
that breaks the
\gamma
-unremovability of watermarkable CCA-secure PKE for some non-negligible
\gamma
, i.e.
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot),\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\cdot)(\mathsf{pk}}
produces some program
C
such that
\Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq\epsilon
for some non-negligible
\epsilon
, whereas
C
satisfies
\Pr[G_{CCA}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk},C)=1]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\gamma
(for
G_{CCA}
see
Section

5.5.2
).
The probability
\Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}]
is taken over the randomness in
\mathsf{WMSetup}
and the probability
\Pr[G_{CCA}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk},C)=1]
is take over the randomness used in
G_{CCA}
.

We will show that we can either break
\gamma^{\prime}
-unremovability of the watermarkable
\mathsf{wPKE}
or CCA-security of
\mathsf{SKE}
.

For any
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})
generated by
\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})
and any
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\cdot),\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\cdot)}(\mathsf{pk})
producing a
\gamma
-good
C
such that
\Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq\epsilon
, one of the following cases must hold:

•

Case 1: the program
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
created during the execution of
\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},C)
is a
\gamma^{\prime}
-good adversary for the stage-2 game
G_{\mathsf{CCA-PKE}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk},\cdot)
for some non-negligible
\gamma^{\prime}
.

The reduction
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{wPKE}}
works as follows:

–

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{wPKE}}
receives
\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}
from the challenger and sends to
\mathcal{A}
.

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{wPKE}}
;

–

for
\mathcal{A}
’s marking queries : query the challenger’s marking oracle
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
and forward the output.

After
\mathcal{A}
outputs program
C
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{wPKE}}
creates circuit
C_{\mathsf{wPKE}}^{\prime}
with black-box access to
C
:

–

C_{\mathsf{wPKE}}^{\prime}
is hardcoded with
\mathsf{pk}
and makes external queries to simulate the decryption oracle for
C
:

*

for
C
’s decryption queries
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
: query the challenger’s decryption oracle
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
; output
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},\mathsf{ct}_{2})
.

–

C
submits challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
submits
(\mathsf{sk}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}),\mathsf{sk}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}))
to the external challenger;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
receives challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
from the challenger and
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
sends

\mathsf{ct}^{**}=(\mathsf{ct}^{*},\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},m_{b}))
to
C
,
where
m_{b}\leftarrow\{m_{0},m_{1}\}
.

–

C
continues to simulate the decryption oracle as above to decrypt only valid ciphertexts.

–

If
C
outputs
b^{\prime}=b
, then
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
outputs guess
0
; else it outputs guess 1.

By the design of out
\mathsf{Extract}
and by our assumption, we must have that
\Pr[\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk},\\
\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{aux},C_{\mathsf{PKE}})\notin{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq\epsilon
where
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
is the program created dring
\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)
.

For any
(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk})
, it is easy to see that program
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
created by
\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)
’s input-output behavior is the same as
C_{\mathsf{wPKE}}^{\prime}
above. Therefore, if
\Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C_{\mathsf{PKE}})\in{\mathcal{Q}}]

is
\gamma^{\prime}
-good and
\Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C_{\mathsf{PKE}})]\geq\epsilon
, so will
C_{\mathsf{wPKE}}^{\prime}
satisfy these two conditions. Thus
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{wPKE}}
breaks the
\gamma^{\prime}
-unremovability.

•

Case 2: the program
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
created during the execution of
\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},C)
is not a
\gamma^{\prime}
-good adversary for the stage-2 game
G_{\mathsf{CCA-PKE}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{pk},\cdot)
for any non-negligible
\gamma^{\prime}
.

In this case, we must have that
|\Pr[C_{\mathsf{PKE}}\to 0|\mathsf{ct}^{*}=\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{0})]-\Pr[C_{\mathsf{PKE}}\to 0|\mathsf{ct}^{*}=\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{1})]=|\Pr[C\text{ outputs }b^{\prime}=b|\mathsf{ct}^{*}=\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{0})]-\Pr[C\text{ outputs }b^{\prime}=b|\mathsf{ct}^{*}=\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{1})]|\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
;

Thus we can switch to doing the following in running
\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},C)
: when
C
submits challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
sends

\mathsf{ct}^{**}=(\mathsf{ct}_{1}^{*}=\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{1}),\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},m_{b}))
to
C
,
where
m_{b}\leftarrow\{m_{0},m_{1}\}
where
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is always
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{1})
.

Since
C
is still
(\gamma-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
good, we will build a reduction to break IND-CCA security of

\mathsf{SKE}
.

The reduction
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{SKE}}
samples
\mathsf{wPKE}
’s keys on its own and can query the oracles
\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\cdot),\\
\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\cdot)
provided by the
\mathsf{SKE}
challenger.

After
\mathcal{A}
outputs
C
,

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{SKE}}
enters stage-2 of reduction and simulates the encryption and decryption oracles by submitting the decryption queries to the encryption, decryption oracles
\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\cdot),\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\cdot)
in the CCA-security game of SKE.
In the challenge phase,

C
submits challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
;
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{SKE}}^{2}
submits
(m_{0},m_{1})
to the external challenger; and samples
\mathsf{sk}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}),
;
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
receives challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},m_{b})
from the challenger, where
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
is only known to the challenger and
C_{\mathsf{PKE}}
sends

\mathsf{ct}^{**}=(\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{1}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{1}),\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},m_{b}))
to
C
.

C
continues to simulate the encryption and decryption oracle as above to decrypt only valid ciphertexts.
In the end, if
C
outputs
b^{\prime}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{SKE}}^{2}
outputs the same
b^{\prime}
.

∎

Appendix F
Watermarkable Functional Encryption from Watermarkable Attribute-Based Encryption

We give a high-level description on how to turn the
[
GKP+13
]
functional encryption construction from ABE, FHE, garbled circuits into a watermarkable FE based on watermarkable ABE. We will not elaborate details of the construction as we cannot possibly cover all explicit constructions for watermarking-compositions of existing schemes, and our main goal here is to give reference to another example of watermarking-composition when applied to an advanced encryption scheme.

All the main algorithms are the same as the
[
GKP+13
]
construction. We will mainly remark on how we do extraction and refer interested readers to
[
GKP+13
]
for the detailed construction.

Watermarkable Functional Encryption: Definitions

A watermarkable
\mathsf{FE}
scheme can be given different
watermarking definitions. One is to watermark the master secret key and given out an unmarked functional key to the adversarial program
\mathcal{A}
; the other is to let
\mathcal{A}
submit a function
f
in the first stage and gets to query on marked versions of the functional
key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
. Both notions are interesting.

For the first notion, we can consider the following security game:
The
\gamma
-Unremovability for a watermarkable single-key FE scheme
says, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, and for all PPT admissible stateful adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
such that:

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{aux}=x,C)\notin{\mathcal{Q}}\\
\wedge C\text{ is }\gamma\text{-good}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{mk})\leftarrow\mathsf{WMSetup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(x,C)\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{msk},\cdot)}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mpk})\\
\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
{\mathcal{Q}}
is the set of marks queried by
\mathcal{A}
and
C=(C_{1},C_{2},D)
is a PPT admissible, stateful
\gamma
-good adversary in the security game
G_{\mathsf{ABE}}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{aux}=(x),\cdot)
if:

\Pr\left[D({\sf st},\mathsf{ct}_{b})=b:\begin{array}[]{cc}(f,{\sf st}_{1})\leftarrow C_{1}(\mathsf{mpk})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)\\
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x),\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathsf{sk}_{f},f,f(x),1^{|x|}),b\leftarrow\{0,1\}\\
{\sf st}\leftarrow C_{2}({\sf st}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{b})\end{array}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\gamma.

where
\mathsf{Sim}
is some PPT simulator.

A selective variant is requiring
\mathcal{A}
output
x
before seeing
\mathsf{mpk}
. Note that we need
\mathcal{A}
to output
x
in the first stage because by relying out construction on ABE, the choice of message
x

will influence the choice of the ABE reduction’s choice of challenge attribute, which must be committed to before entering stage 2.
We can also let
\mathcal{A}
outputs
f
first so that
\mathcal{A}
sees
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
before outputing
x
(which matches the semantics of a fully secure FE).
But then the
\mathsf{Extract}
algorithm will have to take in
f
as an auxiliary inputs.

The case where
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
gets watermarked is slightly trickier to define. We need to work with an indistinguishability-based FE security where the function family
{\mathcal{F}}
is a "high-entropy" function family. Also, the
\mathsf{Extract}
needs to take in the challenge
f

as an auxiliary input. We refer to
[
GKM+19
]
Remark 4.5 for more discussions.
[
GKM+19
]
’s watermarkable predicate encryption
scheme can be extended to a watermarkable FE scheme for watermarking a functional key when working with the above restricted function class.

F.1
Two-Outcome Attribute-Based Encryption

The first building block for
[
GKP+13
]
FE is a two-outcome ABE, called
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
. It has the similar syntax to a normal ABE except that: the encryption scheme takes in
\mathsf{mpk},x,m_{0},m_{1}
; when decrypting with a functional key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
on ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{x,m_{0},m_{1}}
, the decryption will output
m_{b}
when
f(x)=b
.

The single-key
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
security game is exactly like a single-key ABE security game except that
\mathcal{A}
provides 3 challenge messages
(m,m_{0},m_{1})
and attribute
x
, then
\mathcal{A}
is given either
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x,(m_{0},m))
if
f(x)=1
for the key generation query
f
, or

\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x,(m,m_{1}))
if
f(x)=0
, and asked to output
b
.

A single-key
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
scheme can be built with a simple construction from a regular single-key
\mathsf{ABE}
.
We refer the readers to
[
GKP+13
]
Appendix B for details and give a high level idea:
the scheme generates two pairs regular ABE keys; the encryption generates two encryptions of the same message and attribute, each under one of the master public keys respectively; the decryption algorithm on
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
will try to decrypt both ciphertexts, and output that one that has a valid decryption result.

In the watermarking construction, we watermark both the master secret keys of the two
\mathsf{ABE}
(regarding single-key watermarkable ABE security: see
Definition

C.3
except that
\mathcal{A}
is not give
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle and
C
can only query
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
once)).
The
\mathsf{Extract}
algorithm in the watermarkable
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
will turn the input program
C
into a circuit
C_{i}
to break the single-key ABE security for each of the keys in the construction. The reduction in
[
GKP+13
]
Appendix B would go through because
each stage of
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
in the unremovability game would map to the stage of the regular
\mathsf{ABE}
unremovability game. That is, making some
\mathsf{msk}
marking queries after receiving
\mathsf{mpk}
, and outputs challenge messages, attribute and program
C
; then
C
gets to query a single
sk_{f}
and challenged with the challenge ciphertext (If we choose to work with a watermarkable FE scheme where we let
\mathcal{A}
choose
f
, we can also let the adversary in watermarkble
\mathsf{ABE}
scheme query one
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and then the program
C
is not allowed to make any queries).

Watermarkable Functional Encryption Construction, Extraction and Security

We refer the readers to
[
GKP+13
]
Section 3.1 for details on the construction and its preliminaries for definitions of FHE and garbled circuit, since we only plan to give an idea here.

The construction only needs to rely on a watermarkable
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
scheme (which we have shown above can be based on watermarkable regular
\mathsf{ABE}
). The FHE and garbled circuits do not have to be watermarked because they are only generated freshly on each evaluation.

The
\mathsf{WMSetup}
algorithm generates all
\lambda
number of
(\mathsf{mpk}_{i},\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{mk}_{i},\mathsf{xk}_{i})
from the setup of a watermarkable
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
scheme.

The
\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)
algorithm generates a
sk_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}_{f}^{i})
where
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}_{f}^{i}
is outputing the
i
-th bit after the FHE evaluation on some FHE
\mathsf{Eval}
key
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{pk}
, the input function
f
and some FHE ciphertexts
c_{1},\cdots,c_{n}
.

The
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x)
algorithm takes input attribute
x=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
, generates a fresh
\mathsf{FHE}
key pair, encrypt each
x_{i}
to get FHE ciphertext
c_{i}
. Then produce garbled circuit for the FHE decryption algorithm
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{sk},\cdot)
with
2\lambda
labels
\{L_{i}^{0},L_{i}^{1}\}_{i\in[\lambda]}
. Then we produce
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk}_{i},(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{pk},\vec{c}),L_{i}^{0},L_{i}^{1})
for all
i\in[\lambda]
where
\vec{c}=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})
.
Output the ciphertext
(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\cdots\mathsf{ct}_{\lambda})
and the garbled circuit.

The decryption algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct})
runs the
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
decryption on each
\mathsf{ct}_{i}
using
\mathsf{sk}_{i}
to obtain a label
L_{i}^{d_{i}}
, for each
i\in[\lambda]
.
Then one can recover
f(x)\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{sk},d_{1}\cdots d_{\lambda})
by using the labels
L_{i}^{d_{i}}
and the garbled circuit.

Now we can talk about how we watermark and extract: the marking scheme simply marks each
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}
for
i\in[\lambda]
. To see how we extract, we roughly open up the security proof of the above scheme: On an input program
C
, the extraction interacts with
C
as in the stage-2 FE game in our unremovability definition. Recall that in the end of the original security game, we need to prepare either real ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},x)
or a simulated ciphertext, and ask the final stage adversary
D
to distinguish. But in our extraction algorithm that tries to create circuit
C_{i}
to break the unremovability of the
i
-the
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
scheme, we instead sample either of the following: (1) generate the ciphertext as in the original scheme; (2) use the label
L_{i}^{d_{i}}
twice in the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{i}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}_{2}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk}_{i},(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{pk},\vec{c}),L_{i}^{d_{i}},L_{i}^{d_{i}})
, where
d_{i}=\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}_{f}^{i}(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{pk},\vec{c})
.
More specifically, circuit
C_{i}
obtains one of these ciphetexts from some external
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
challenger and uses
C
’s output to distinguish them.

Why would a
\gamma
-good
C
in the original security game still be
\gamma
-good when we simulate the game as in the above extraction?
This relies on the hybrid arguments invoking the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{FHE}
and then the security of garbled circuit. We refer the details to the proofs for Hybrid 0 to Hybrid 2 in Section 3.2 of
[
GKP+13
]
. By invoking these security properties, we move from giving a completely simulated ciphertext, to give the fake ciphertext described in the extraction algorithm (when flipping the coin to
1
), while
C
should still be
\gamma
-good with some noticeable probability.

Then we can argue that at least one of
C_{i}
is
\gamma_{i}
-good for some non negligible
\gamma_{i}
, we can break unremovability of
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
with the
i
-th key pair.
In the marking stage, the
i
-th
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
reduction can simulate the marking queries by making marking query to the
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}
marking oracle (and mark the other keys on its own).
After
\mathcal{A}
commits to chellenge message
x
, the reduction can also commit the challenge attribute
(\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{pk},\vec{c})
where
\vec{c}
is computed from
x
as in the construction.
After
\mathcal{A}
outputs
C
, the reduction creates circuit
C_{i}
as in the extraction algorithm, and
C_{i}
can answer
C
’s single key query by making a single query to the
\mathsf{ABE}_{2}

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
oracle.

Appendix G
Watermarkable Non-malleable PKE from Watermarkable CPA-secure PKE, NIZK and Signatures

G.1
Watermarkable Implementation of Non-malleable CCA2 PKE

We present that an alternative construction to
Appendix

A
, the CCA2 non-malleable PKE in
[
DDN91
]
also has a watermarking implementation. We will omit the security proof due to the similarity to
Appendix

A
.

G.2
Construction and Security

Our construction is based on the non-malleable encryption scheme in
[
DDN91
]
.

Given a watermarkable implementation of a CPA-secure PKE
\mathsf{wPKE}=(\mathsf{WMSetup},\\
\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Extract})
, a NIZK scheme
\mathsf{NIZK}=(\mathsf{Setup},{\sf Prove},\mathsf{Verify})
and a signature scheme
{\sf DS}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},{\sf Sign},\mathsf{Verify})
. where the verification key size of
{\sf DS}
is
n
.

Construction

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},1^{n}):

For
i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}
:
compute
(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,b},\mathsf{xk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk}_{i,b};\mathsf{mk}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{mk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{WMSetup}(\lambda)
;

Compute
\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{NIZK}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
;
Output
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,b})_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}};\mathsf{pk}=(\{\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}},\mathsf{CRS});\mathsf{xk}=\{\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}};\mathsf{mk}=\{\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)
:

•

parse
\mathsf{pk}:=\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}},\mathsf{CRS}
; compute
({\sf DS}.\mathsf{sk},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow{\sf DS}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
;
Let us denote
i
-th bit of
{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk}
as
\mathsf{vk}_{i}
.

•

For each
i\in[n]
: compute

\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,\mathsf{vk}_{i}},m)
;

•

compute
\pi\leftarrow\mathsf{NIZK}.{\sf Prove}(\mathsf{CRS},(\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{2},\cdots,\mathsf{ct}_{n},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk}),(r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{n},m))
for the following statement:

\exists
witness
(r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{n},m)
such that
\mathsf{ct}_{i}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,\mathsf{vk}_{i}},m;r_{i})
for all
i\in[n]
, where
r_{i}
is the randomness used in encryption.

•

compute a signature
{\sf sig}\leftarrow{\sf DS}.{\sf Sign}({\sf DS}.\mathsf{sk},(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in n},\pi))

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in n},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk},\pi,{\sf sig})
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})
:

•

parse
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in n},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk},\pi);\mathsf{sk}=\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
;

•

if
{\sf DS}.\mathsf{Verify}({\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk},(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in n},\pi),{\sf sig})=1
continue; else abort and output
\bot
.

•

if
\mathsf{NIZK}.\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{CRS},\pi,((\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in n},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk}))=1
, continue; else abort and output
\bot
.

•

compute
m\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{1,\mathsf{vk}_{1}},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
;
output
m^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{mk},\mathsf{sk},\tau)
: parse
\mathsf{mk}=\{\mathsf{mk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}};\mathsf{sk}=\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
; output
\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,b,\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}_{i,b}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{sk}_{i,b},\tau)\}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
;

\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{xk},\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{aux}=(m_{0},m_{1}),C)
:

•

parse
\mathsf{xk}:=(\{\mathsf{xk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}},\mathsf{NIZK}.\mathsf{td});\mathsf{pk}:=(\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}},\mathsf{CRS})
.
Initialize an empty tuple
\vec{\tau}
;

For
i^{*}=1,\cdots,n;b^{*}=0,1
:
Create the following circuit
C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
with black-box access to
C
:

–

C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
is hardcoded with
(\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}},\{\mathsf{xk}_{i,b}\}{i\neq{i^{*}}\vee b\neq b^{*}},\mathsf{td},\mathsf{CRS})

C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
acts as a stage-2 reduction from CCA2-PKE to CPA-PKE with the keys
(\mathsf{sk}_{{i^{*}},b^{*}},\mathsf{pk}_{{i^{*}},b^{*}})
simulates the CCA2-PKE stage-2 game for
C
as follows:

–

For
C
’s decryption queries
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i,\mathsf{vk}_{i}}\},\mathsf{vk},\pi,{\sf sig})
:

*

First check if
\mathsf{NIZK}.\mathsf{Verify}(\mathsf{CRS},\pi)=1
, if 0 output
\bot
; else continue;

*

By the extraction key simulation property, since
\mathsf{xk}_{i\neq{i^{*}}\vee b\neq b^{*}}
can be used to simulate the oracles used in
G_{CPA}(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{j},\cdot)
for
i\neq{i^{*}}
or
b\neq b^{*}
,
C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
can simulate the oracle
\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{{i^{*}},1-b^{*}},\cdot)
and thus decrypt
\mathsf{ct}_{{i^{*}},1-b^{*}}
in the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
to output
m
.

–

C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
submits
(m_{0},m_{1})
to the external challenger;
C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
receives challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}^{*}=\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{{i^{*}},b^{*}},m_{\delta}),\delta\leftarrow\{0,1\}
from the challenger.

–

C_{{i^{*}},b^{*}}
prepares the following ciphertext:

1.

Sample
({\sf DS}.\mathsf{vk}^{*},{\sf DS}.\mathsf{sk}^{*})\leftarrow{\sf DS}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
so that the
i^{*}
-th bit of
\mathsf{vk}^{*}
is
b^{*}
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{ct}_{i,b_{i}}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,b_{i}},m_{\delta^{\prime}}),\delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
for all
i\neq{i^{*}}
, where
b_{i}=\mathsf{vk}^{*}_{i}
.

3.

compute
\widehat{\pi}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{td},\mathsf{CRS},
,
(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i,b_{i}}^{*}\}_{i\in[n],b_{i}=\mathsf{vk}^{*}_{i}}))

where
\mathsf{Sim}
is the simulator algorithm for
\mathsf{NIZK}
.

Then it sends

\mathsf{ct}^{*}=(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i,b_{i}}^{*}\}_{i\in[n],b_{i}=\mathsf{vk}^{*}_{i}}),\mathsf{vk}^{*},\widehat{\pi})
to
C
.

–

C
continues to simulate the decryption oracle as above to decrypt only valid ciphertexts
\mathsf{ct}\neq\mathsf{ct}^{*}
, except adding the following check:

*

Check if
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{vk}^{*}
, if yes, output
\bot
; else continue to decrypt.

–

C
outputs
\ell
ciphertexts
\mathsf{ct}_{1},\cdots,\mathsf{ct}_{\ell}
and
C_{i}
computes
d_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}_{j})
if
ct_{j}\neq\mathsf{ct}^{*}
, else
d_{j}=\bot
.

–

In the end, feed
(d_{1},\cdots,d_{\ell})
to
C
and
C
outputs a bit b’.

C_{i}
outputs the same as

C
outputs.

–

Add
\tau/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{xk}_{i^{*},b^{*}},\mathsf{wPKE}.\mathsf{pk}_{i^{*},b^{*}},\mathsf{aux}=(m_{0},m_{1}),C_{i^{*},b^{*}})
to
\vec{\tau}
.

•

Output
\vec{\tau}
.

The security proof is highly similar to
Appendix

A
and we omit it here.
